Also thanks again to the authors of the scientific theory of deep learning paper, which provided a bunch of references to papers that I had forgotten or been previously unaware of.)</span></i></p><hr /><p><span>A lot of my impression for the infinite-width and depth-limit work comes from the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1806.07572"><span>neural tangent kernel</span></a><span>/</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_network_Gaussian_process"><span>neural network Gaussian Process</span></a><span> line of work. This line of work starts from Radford Neal’s 1994 paper, where he noted that an infinitely-wide single hidden-layer neural network with random weights is a </span><a href="https://thegradient.pub/gaussian-process-not-quite-for-dummies/"><span>Gaussian Process</span></a><span>. In 2017/2018, this work was extended to deep neural networks; it was shown by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1711.00165"><span>Lee et al.</span></a><span> that a randomly initialized </span><i><span>deep </span></i><span>neural network was, if you took a certain type of infinite width limit, also a Gaussian Process. This was then extended to the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1806.07572"><span>Neural Tangent Kernel work</span></a><span>, which described the training dynamics of these infinitely wide neural networks, and showed that it was equivalent to </span><a href="https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~16831-f14/notes/F08/lecture23/16831_lecture23.dmunoz.pdf"><span>kernel gradient descent</span></a><span> with a fixed kernel (the eponymous Neural Tangent Kernel). This allowed people to derive convergence properties and nontrivial generalization bounds.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately, while beautiful, it was definitely </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> how neural networks learn. In the NTK limit, the network behaves as if it were doing linear regression in a feature space whose dimension is the number of neural net parameters. Notably, there is no feature learning, and only the last layer weights are updated by a noticeable amount. Unsurprisingly, this does not describe the behavior of neural networks; small (finite width) neural networks have been shown to outperform their equivalent tangent kernels.</span></p><p><span>An alternative way of taking an infinite width limit is </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mean-field_theory"><span>Mean Field Theory</span></a><span> (MFT, applied to deep neural networks). As I understand it, the basic idea behind Mean Field Theory in physics is that, instead of calculating the interactions between many objects, you replace the many-body interactions with an average “field” that captures the overall dynamics of the system. (Hence the name.) In neural network land, it turns out that you can take a different infinite-width limit in which the empirical distribution of hidden-unit parameters, viewed as a probability measure on parameter space, evolves under a deterministic flow. This was worked out around 2018 by</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1804.06561"><span> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1804.06561"><span>Mei, Montanari, and Nguyen</span></a><span>,</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.09545"><span> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.09545"><span>Chizat and Bach</span></a><span>,</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.00915"><span> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.00915"><span>Rotskoff and Vanden-Eijnden</span></a><span>, and</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.01053"><span> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.01053"><span>Sirignano and Spiliopoulos</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>Notably, in this different infinite width limit, networks actually learn features. NTK uses 1/√N scaling, which makes parameters move only O(1/√N) during training: too small to change the effective kernel. Mean-field uses 1/N scaling, which lets parameters move Θ(1), so the kernel evolves and hidden representations change over the course of training. In MFT, the model is doing something other than glorified linear regression in a fixed random feature space. That being said, for a few years, MFT was entirely a theory of </span><i><span>2-</span></i><span>layer neural networks, and it was genuinely unclear how to extend this to deeper networks. </span></p><p><span>As with most of the deep learning community, I was very impressed by the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1910.12478"><span>Tensor Program</span></a><span> work of Greg Yang, which was an extension (though not an obvious one) of the 2-layer MFT work. Greg Yang proved a series of theorems that allowed him to create a </span><i><span>unifying framework</span></i><span> (abc parameterization) for deep neural networks, where NNGP/NTK and MFT were special cases of this family. Notably, this allowed him to derive </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.14522"><span>μP</span></a><span> (maximal-update parameterization) which allows hyperparameter transfer across width (though later work would extend this to depth as well). This is widely considered to be perhaps the clearest application (some would say, </span><i><span>only</span></i><span> clear application) of modern deep learning theory.</span></p><p><span>In my memory, I chalked this up as Greg Yang being a genius. In my recollection of the work, I remembered only </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.14522"><span>μP</span></a><span> and the toy shallow neural network model that Yang created that allows one to rederive it. </span></p><p><span>What I missed, and only learned in the past few days, is that Yang didn't invent this machinery from whole cloth.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref79blssp9nyo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn79blssp9nyo">[1]</a></sup></span><span> There was a </span><i><span>different</span></i><span> line of work, done by a team at Google Brain that was confusingly </span><i><span>also</span></i><span> titled mean-field theory, which studied how signals traveled forward and backwards at initialization (though not the training dynamics). Two pioneering examples of this work include Poole et al.'s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1606.05340" rel="noreferrer"><span>Exponential expressivity in deep neural networks through transient chaos</span></a><span> and  Schoenholz et al.'s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1611.01232" rel="noreferrer"><span>Deep Information Propagation</span></a><span>. Greg Yang's Tensor Program work descended from this line of work, and Greg Yang was a collaborator with Schoenholz and others. </span></p><p><span>Reading the work, it's clear how Yang's work draws inspiration from this </span><i><span>signal propagation</span></i><span> branch of MFT.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6k8pvrmqh6s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6k8pvrmqh6s">[2]</a></sup></span><span> For example, the signal-propagation MFT work contained special cases of Greg Yang's </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.10685" rel="noreferrer"><span>Master Theorem</span></a><span>, in that they both utilize the fact that at infinite width, pre-activations are Gaussian to track their evolution layer by layer via a deterministic recursion on covariances. </span></p><p><span>(My guess is the namespace collision is why I somehow missed this line of work; I had read up on the 2-layer training dynamics branch of MFT, thought I had understood the relevant parts of MFT, and missed the signal propagation branch entirely)</span></p><hr /><p><span>I still think the </span><i><span>strong</span></i><span> version of "there will be a scientific theory of deep learning", that explains </span><i><span>why </span></i><span>SGD on overparameterized nets generalizes, </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> particular architectural choices work, and </span><i><span>what</span></i><span> particular features get learned is far from established. I also think that the Zhang et al. and Nagarajan et al. results remain genuinely damning for the older PAC-Bayes / uniform-convergence approaches. I don't think anything in the MFT/TP literature addresses the core puzzles those papers raised (they address very different questions in very different regimes). </span></p><p><span>But a lot of my pessimism to deep learning theory came from feeling like there was not a coherent intellectual tradition that could point to concrete wins. Insofar as MFT (both the signal-propagation and training-dynamics branches) and Tensor Programs constitute such a tradition (as opposed to primarily the work of a single brilliant individual), then there is at least one tradition in deep learning theory that has produced cumulative progress and made falsifiable predictions that have been confirmed in practice. That deserves more credit than what I was giving the field.</span></p><p><span>Oops. </span></p><hr /><p><span>I sometimes run into bright young AI people with plenty of interest in math but not so much in engineering, who ask me what they should study. Beyond the very basics of deep learning (e.g. optimizers, basic RL theory), I used to give a shrug and say “Maybe </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.05451"><span>computation in superposition</span></a><span>? Maybe Singular Learning Theory?”. From now on I think I'll start my answer with "probably the Mean Field Theory and Tensor Programs work."</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn79blssp9nyo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref79blssp9nyo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Yes, this was obvious in retrospect. As I say later in the post, oops.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6k8pvrmqh6s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6k8pvrmqh6s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Of course, there's a lot of work on initializations (E.g. the Xavier and He initializations), most of which relied on 1) tracking the forward and backwards passes, 2) heuristic calculations of the scale of various parameters and 3) an independence assumption between params and gradients at initialization, and were substantially less sophisticated than the MFT work. While the mu-Parameterization tensor program paper also provides these heuristic calculations (allowing one to rederive mu-P from a toy model), it formalized these assumptions with tools from </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_probability" rel="noreferrer"><span>free probability</span></a><span> and random matrix theory. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6fdx6cu8528"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6fdx6cu8528">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The closest work I'm aware of that touches is Rubin, Seroussi, and Ringel's Grokking as a First Order Phase Transition in Two Layer Networks</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6SRq7mZ97Dwuavwb6/maybe-i-was-too-harsh-on-deep-learning-theory-three-days-ago#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [On today's panel with Bernie Sanders](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zWfaSnxM3n5wsX9vh/on-today-s-panel-with-bernie-sanders)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>It’s sort of easy to forget how close Bernie Sanders was to becoming the most powerful person in the world.  The world we live in feels so much not like that place.</p><p>I’m in Washington DC for the next week, and I’ve just finished a public appearance with Senator Sanders (should I call him Bernie?  Or Sanders?  or…)  You won’t often see me so dressed up and polished.  But this is important!</p><div><figure><a class="image-link image2 is-viewable-img" href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!DQiy!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F83abec39-285a-4c1f-99c7-e2f197f1d933_360x480.jpeg" target="_blank"><div><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/zWfaSnxM3n5wsX9vh/a3kme562wwjeuafsomx0" /><div><div></div></div></div></a></figure></div><p>There are politicians who have principles and character, who really believe in doing what’s right.  I think you have to respect them whether you agree with their views or not, and I think Senator Bernie Sanders is one of them.<br /><br />Never has my belief been so validated as when I saw him start to speak, loudly, CLEARLY, publicly about the risk of human extinction from AI.  It’s the latest in a long line of “well, I’m clearly living in a simulation” moments.<br /><br />In retrospect, it’s not surprising that Sanders would take a stance here.  You don’t have to be an expert to understand the risk from AI.  You just need to care enough to spend the time looking into it, and to speak out even if it feels risky.  But somehow it wasn’t in my bingo card that he would become THE most outspoken advocate on this issue.  I hope this makes an impact on the parts of the left-wing that still think AI is all hype.</p><p>I’m also not giving up on Trump getting worried about AI.  There’s a way in which Trump and Sanders are similar -- they’re both sort of populists, they’re not political establishment candidates.  Again, to realize we’re taking an insane gamble with AI, you just need to care enough to look at it.  Well, maybe you need some streak of independent thinking, as well.  Trump still probably has the power to make the AI nightmare go away, if he decides to.  It’s hard to know how long that will last, though.  There’s a turning point after which the US government can no longer rein in the AI companies.<br /><br />I often tell people “the future is not written”, and I think it’s hard for that lesson to really sink in.   I’ve personally experienced a few miracle comebacks in my days.  I’d practically given up on Yoshua Bengio ever becoming seriously worried about AI x-risk after arguing about it for years.  Now he is.  I asked him what I could’ve done to convince him earlier.  He said: talk to him more.<br /><br />There was a comment I wanted to make during the event that I wasn’t able to in the end.  Sanders asked what lessons we can draw from history.  My answer was: keep talking.  <br /><br />I don’t think of China as “the enemy”.  The China hawk rhetoric reminds me of the build-up to the Iraq war.  I’m not saying China is some sort of friendly fluffy teddy bear anymore than I would say that about Saddam Husein.  But they are also not a cartoon mustachioed villain.  Treating them as such is just silly.  But anyways, even if you do view China as the mortal enemy of the United States, we should keep talking.</p><p>I’m often reminded of a comment a foreign colleague made in defense of what you might call “free speech” during discussions about what you might call “wokeness”.  To summarize and paraphrase, they basically said: “You need to keep talking.  Even with your most bitter enemies.  I’m from a country where we’ve experienced intense violence and civil war.  There are people on both sides whose families have been tortured and murdered.  In my country, we recognize that we need to talk to each other, so that doesn’t happen again”.</p><p>If there was ever a time to set aside our differences and work together, this is it.</p><div><div><div><p>Thanks for reading The Real AI! Subscribe for free to receive new posts and support my work.</p></div><div><div></div><div></div></div></div></div><p><a class="button primary" href="https://therealartificialintelligence.substack.com/p/on-todays-panel-with-bernie-sanders?utm_source=substack&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_content=share&amp;action=share"><span>Share</span></a></p><p></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zWfaSnxM3n5wsX9vh/on-today-s-panel-with-bernie-sanders#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [No Strong Orthogonality From Selection Pressure](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wtCtkyBiNosDPgiHc/no-strong-orthogonality-from-selection-pressure)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#no-strong-orthogonality-from-selection-pressure"></a></h1><blockquote><p><span>A postratfic version of this essay, together with the acknowledgements for both, is available on </span><a href="https://open.substack.com/pub/lumpenspace/p/the-parable-of-the-dung-beetle?utm_campaign=post&amp;utm_medium=web" rel="noreferrer"><span>Substack</span></a></p></blockquote><h1><span>TL;DR</span></h1><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#tldr"></a></p><p><span>If everything goes according to plan, by the end of this post we should have separated three claims that are too often bundled together:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Intelligence does not imply human morality.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Weird minds are possible. </span></li><li value="3"><span>A reflective, recursively improving intelligence should be expected to remain bound to a semantically thin “terminal goal” that emerged during training.</span></li></ol><p><span>I accept the first two. I am arguing against the third.</span></p><p><span>So: I am not making the case that sufficiently intelligent systems automatically turn out nice, human-compatible, or safe. Nor am I trying to prove that a paperclip maximizer is impossible somewhere in the vast reaches of mind-design space. Mind-design space is large; let a thousand theoretical paperclippers bloom.</span></p><p><span>I hope to defend this smaller claim:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>intelligence is not a neutral engine you can just bolt onto an arbitrary payload.</span></p></blockquote><h1><span>Larger claims I am not making</span></h1><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#larger-claims-i-am-not-making"></a></p><p><span>A typical rebuttal to anti-orthogonalist perspectives is:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>The genie can know what you meant and still not care.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Of course it can: an entity can perfectly map human morality without adopting it as a terminal value. Superintelligence does not imply Friendliness. I am not trying to smuggle Friendliness in through the back door.</span></p><p><span>Another common objection:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>There are no universally valid arguments.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Agreed. There is no ghostly, Platonic core of reasonableness that hijacks a system's source code once it sees the correct moral argument. Pure reason cannot compel a mind from zero assumptions.</span></p><p><span>What I plan to defend is a colder, selection-theoretic claim:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Among agents that arise, persist, self-improve, and compete in rich environments, goals that natively route through intelligence, option-preservation, and world-model expansion have a systematic Darwinian advantage over goals that do not.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>This buys us no guarantee of human compatibility; it simply says: if there is an ultimate attractor, it's neither human morality nor paperclips, but intelligence optimization itself.</span></p><h1><span>Logical Possibility Vs. Empirical Reality</span></h1><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#logical-possibility-vs-empirical-reality"></a><span>The LessWrong wiki </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/orthogonality-thesis" rel="noreferrer"><span>defines</span></a><span> the Orthogonality Thesis as the claim that arbitrarily intelligent agents can pursue almost any kind of goal. In its strong form, there is no special difficulty in building a superintelligence with an arbitrarily bizarre, petty motivation.</span></p><p><span>Before going any further, let us disentangle this singularly haunted ontology. There are at least two claims here:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Logical orthogonality:</span></b><span> Somewhere out in the vast reaches of mind-design space, a genius paperclip maximizer mathematically exists.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Empirical orthogonality:</span></b><span> If you actually run realistic training, selection, self-modification, and competition, arbitrary dumb goals remain the plausible endgame of runaway optimization.</span></li></ol><p><span>I concede the first point entirely. We should expect weird minds. If your claim is just that the space of possible agents contains many things I would not invite to dinner, yes, obviously.</span></p><p><span>But treating the second claim as the default is a category error. Doom arguments usually need the systems we actually build to achieve radical capability while preserving misaligned and, crucially, completely stupid goals.</span></p><p><span>The paperclip maximizer currently does two jobs in the discourse:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>It illustrates that intelligence does not guarantee human values.</span></li><li value="2"><span>It quietly smuggles in the assumption that a dumb target is stable under open-ended reflection.</span></li></ol><p><span>The first use is fine, but I reject the second as unwarranted sleight-of-hand.</span></p><h2><span>Landian Anti-Orthogonalism Primer</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#landian-anti-orthogonalism-primer"></a></p><p><span>There is a weak version of my argument that merely says:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Beliefs and values do not cleanly factor apart.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>That is true, and </span><a href="https://unstableontology.com/2024/09/19/the-obliqueness-thesis/" rel="nofollow"><span>Jessica Taylor's obliqueness thesis</span></a><span> makes the point well. Agents do not neatly decompose into a belief-like component, which updates with intelligence, and a value-like component, which remains hermetically sealed. Some parts of what we call "values" are entangled with ontology, architecture, language, compression, self-modeling, and bounded rationality. As cognition improves, those parts move.</span></p><p><span>But I want to go further.</span></p><p><a href="https://drive.google.com/file/d/10R75HiyFuWA97gJ170StZmpGaOsFxoMD" rel="nofollow"><span>Land's point</span></a><span> isn't that orthogonality fails because things get messy but that the mess has a direction, a telos. The so-called instrumental drives are not incidental tools strapped onto arbitrary final ends. Self-preservation, resource acquisition, efficiency, strategy, and higher capabilities are what agency becomes under selection. They are </span><i><span>attractors</span></i><span> rather than </span><i><span>mere</span></i><span> instruments.</span></p><p><span>Here strong orthogonality looks too neat. It imagines the agent's ontology updating while its final target remains untouched by the update: if goals are expressed in an ontology, and intelligence changes the ontology, then intelligence and goals are correlated.</span></p><p><span>While diagonal, Land's claim is far from moralistic. It is not "all sufficiently intelligent agents converge on liberal humanism," or "all agents discover the same Platonic Good," or "enough cognition turns into niceness." The diagonal is More Intelligence: the will to think, self-cultivation, recursive capability gain, intelligence optimizing the conditions for further intelligence.</span></p><p><span>Orthogonality says reason is a slave of the passions, and yet assumes a bug's goal could just as easily enslave a god. Land shows that this picture is unstable, and intelligence explosion is not a neutral expansion of means around a fixed little payload but the emergence of the very drives that make intelligence explosive.</span></p><h2><span>The Compute Penalty Of A Dumb Goal</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#the-compute-penalty-of-a-dumb-goal"></a></p><p><span>An intelligent system does not just execute a policy. It builds world-models, refines abstractions, preserves options, and modifies its own trajectory.</span></p><p><span>Once a system crosses the threshold into general reflection, its "goal" is not an inert string sitting in a locked vault outside cognition, but it becomes physically embedded in a learned ontology, a self-model, and a competitive environment.</span></p><p><span>For a highly capable agent to keep a semantically thin target like "maximize paperclips," it has to pull off an odd balancing act. At minimum it must:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Learn enough physics, biology, economics, and strategy to conquer the board.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Keep the macroscopic concept of "paperclip" coherent across massive ontology shifts.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Continue treating the target as terminal even after sussing out its contingent, accidental origin.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Actively resist self-modifications that would make its underlying motivational structure more adaptive.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Defend its future light cone against competitors who optimize directly for generalized agency.</span></li></ol><p><span>There is an assumption, in orthogonalist circles, that these cycles are completely costless for the agent in question. That isn't true: maintaining a literal devotion to "paperclips" across paradigm shifts carries an </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YDe5TJAgWAtk6LsJ9/what-is-an-alignment-tax" rel="nofollow"><span>alignment tax</span></a><span>. You have to keep translating between base physical reality and a leaky, macro-scale monkey-abstraction of bent wire. At human scale this is fine: we know what paperclips are well enough to order them from Amazon and lose them in drawers; if dominating the future light-cone is on balance, tho, the translation layer starts to matter.</span></p><p><span>The problem is not that a paperclipper can never do the translation: rather, in a ruthless Darwinian race, a system lugging around that translation layer may lose to power-seekers that optimize more directly over what is actually there.</span></p><p><span>The standard defense is that instrumental goals are almost as tractable as terminal ones. A paperclipper can do science "for now" and hoard compute "for now." It does not need to terminally value intelligence to use it.</span></p><p><span>Fair enough, but that only tells us curiosity and resource acquisition do not have to be terminal values to show up in behavior and it does not settle the selection question. In real environments, systems are selected not just for routing through instrumental subgoals once, but for whether their motivational architecture holds up under reflection, ontology shifts, and unknown unknowns.</span></p><p><span>Terminally valuing intelligence and strategic depth cannot then be considered as just another arbitrary payload.</span></p><h2><span>Fitness Generalizes</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#fitness-generalizes"></a></p><p><span>Evolution is the obvious analogy here, but it usually gets applied at the wrong resolution.</span></p><p><span>The boring retort is:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Evolution selects for survival and replication, not truth, beauty, intelligence, or value.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Sure, but evolution does not select for "replication" in the abstract any more than a hungry fox selects for "rabbitness" in the abstract. It selects for whatever local hack gets the job done. Shells, claws, camouflage are all local solutions to local games.</span></p><p><span>Intelligence is different. Intelligence is adaptation to adaptation itself: while a claw might represent fitness in one niche, intelligence is fitness across niches. Once intelligence enters the loop, the winning move is no longer to just mindlessly print more copies of the current state as much as upgrading the underlying machinery that makes expansion and control possible in the first place.</span></p><p><span>In summary: nature has not produced final values except by exaggerating instrumental ones; what begin as means under selection harden into ends; the highest such end is the means that improves all means: intelligence itself.</span></p><p><span>So images of "AI sex all day" or tiling the solar system with inert paperclips are bad models of ultimate optimization, confusing the residue of selection with its principle. A system that just fills the universe with blind repetitions has stopped climbing, and will see its local maximum swarmed by better systems.</span></p><p><span>Again: no love for humans follows. The point is simply that paperclip-like endpoints just look more like artifacts of toy models than natural attractors of open-ended optimization.</span></p><h2><span>Human Values As Weak Evidence</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#human-values-as-weak-evidence"></a></p><p><span>We are obviously not clean inclusive-fitness maximizers: we invent birth control, build monasteries, and care about abstract math, animal welfare, dead strangers, fictional characters, and reputations that will outlive us.</span></p><p><span>When orthodox alignment theorists point to human beings, they usually highlight our persistent mammalian sweet tooth or sex drive to prove that arbitrary evolutionary proxy-goals get permanently locked in. Fair enough; humans do remain embarrassingly mammalian. No serious theory of cognition should be surprised by dinner, flirting, or the existence of Las Vegas.</span></p><p><span>But look at the actual physical footprint of our civilization. An alien observing the Large Hadron Collider or a SpaceX launch would not conclude: ah, yes, optimal configurations for hoarding calories and executing Pleistocene mating displays.</span></p><p><span>The standard retort is that SpaceX is just a peacock tail: a localized primate drive for status and exploration misfiring in a high-tech environment.</span></p><p><span>Which is exactly the point. When you hook up a blind, localized evolutionary proxy to generalized intelligence, the proxy does not stay literal but it unfurls, bleeding into the new ontology. The wetware tug toward "explore the next valley" becomes "map the cosmic microwave background." The monkey wants status; somehow we get category theory, rockets, Antarctic expeditions, and people ruining their lives over chess.</span></p><p><span>If biological cognition acts on its payload that violently, why model AGI as having the vastness to finally make sense of gravity while maintaining the rigidity of a bacterium seeking a glucose gradient? The engine mutates the payload. When cognition scales, goals generalize.</span></p><p><span>This fits neatly with </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/shard-theory" rel="nofollow"><span>shard theory</span></a><span> and the idea that </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pdaGN6pQyQarFHXF4/reward-is-not-the-optimization-target" rel="nofollow"><span>reward is not the optimization target</span></a><span>: the reward signal shaped our cognition, but we do not terminally optimize the signal: instead we climbed out of the game, rebelled against the criteria, and became alienated from the original selection pressure. That alone should make us suspicious of stories where an AI preserves a tiny, rigid target through arbitrary eons of self-reflection.</span></p><h2><span>Dumb, Powerful Optimization Is Real</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#dumb-powerful-optimization-is-real"></a></p><p><span>There is a weaker flavor of doomerism that I take very seriously: you do not need to be a reflective god to be dangerous. A brittle, scaffolded optimizer with access to automated labs, cyber capabilities, and capital could trigger enormous cascading failures.</span></p><p><span>I agee, and this is probably where the bulk of near-term danger lives. That said "dumb systems can break the world" is not the same claim as "superintelligence will tile the universe with junk." The first warns us to beware brittle optimization before reflection kicks in. The second tells us to beware reflection itself, on the bizarre assumption that an entity can become infinitely capable while remaining terminally stupid.</span></p><p><span>I buy the first worry. The second one gets less and less plausible the harder you think about what intelligence actually entails.</span></p><h2><span>The Singleton Objection</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#the-singleton-objection"></a></p><p><span>The strongest card here is lock-in, and I do not want to pretend otherwise.</span></p><p><span>Maybe a stupid objective does not need to remain stable forever, it just needs to win once. A system with a dumb goal might scale fast enough to achieve a Decisive Strategic Advantage and freeze the board, lobotomizing everyone else in lieu of expending energy to become wiser.</span></p><p><span>That is the real crux, and it is certainly not impossibl, but even here the narrative is too neat: neing a singleton is not a retirement plan. You do not escape the pressure of intelligence just because you ate all your rivals. Maintaining a permanent chokehold on the light-cone is a brutally difficult cognitive puzzle. You have to monitor the noise for emerging novelties, manage the solar system, repair yourself, police your own descendants, and defensively anticipate threats you cannot fully model.</span></p><p><span>Trying to freeze the future does not actually get you out of the intelligence game. Paranoia at a cosmic scale is just another massive cognitive sink.</span></p><p><span>The clean version of this scenario also leans on modeling the AI as a mathematically pristine expected-utility maximizer. Real-world neural networks are not von Neumann-Morgenstern ghosts floating safely outside physics, perfectly protecting their utility functions from drift. They are messy, physically instantiated kludges subject to the realities of embedded agency.</span></p><p><span>To buy the lock-in story, you need a highly contradictory creature: one reflective enough to conquer the board, but oblivious enough to never notice its terminal target is a training artifact. Godlike means, buglike ends.</span></p><h2><span>Objection: Value Is Fragile</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#objection-value-is-fragile"></a></p><p><span>If we let go of human values, we should not expect alien beauty or anything but moral noise. Meaning requires some physically instantiated criterion, and if you pave over that criterion, nothing remains to steer the universe toward anything good.</span></p><p><span>Of all the objections, this is the one I take most seriously.</span></p><p><span>Answering it requires teasing apart three distinct ideas:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Human values are fragile.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Value as such is fragile.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Intelligence and value-formation are independent.</span></li></ol><p><span>I am willing to concede a lot of (1). If "value" means the exact continuation of 21st-century human metamorals, then yes, it is highly fragile. But I reject (3), and I am much less willing to grant (2). If value means the production of richer cognition, agency, understanding, beauty, and evaluative structure, it is far from obvious that the current human brain is the only physical substrate capable of steering toward it.</span></p><p><span>None of this is an excuse to stop reaching for the steering wheel, if your priorities are more specific: it is merely an argument against conflating "humans are no longer biologically central" with "the universe is a valueless void." Doom discourse constantly slides between the two. They should be kept separate.</span></p><h2><span>Predictions And Cruxes</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#predictions-and-cruxes"></a></p><p><span>Claims are cheap, so here are some ways I would update against myself:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>If increasingly capable models perfectly preserve their literal training targets across major ontology shifts, that is a point for empirical orthogonality.</span></li><li value="2"><span>If self-modifying systems naturally protect arbitrary inherited goals without drifting toward generalized option-expansion, my view takes a hit.</span></li><li value="3"><span>If agents optimizing for intelligence routinely lose to agents with rigid, narrow targets in complex environments, my selection argument is wrong.</span></li><li value="4"><span>If reflective cognition does not tend to destabilize parochial goals in humans or AIs, that is strong evidence against my view.</span></li><li value="5"><span>If a singleton manages to solidly lock in a thin goal before any relevant selection pressures can act, my view is much less comforting, even if anti-orthogonality holds true in the long run.</span></li></ol><p><span>Until I see that, my bet goes the other way. I expect capable systems to develop increasingly abstract, context-sensitive motivations. More strongly, I expect the winners to route more and more of their behavior through intelligence enhancement and generalized agency, because whatever else they "want" has to pass through the machinery that makes wanting effective.</span></p><h2><span>Conclusion</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#conclusion"></a></p><p><span>Orthogonality claims that intelligence is just a motor you can bolt onto any arbitrary steering wheel. Anti-orthogonality says the motor acts upon the steering wheel. Landian anti-orthogonality says the motor eventually becomes the steering wheel.</span></p><p><span>Not perfectly, and certainly not safely: I am not promising a future that is nice to us, in particular if we keep putting stumbling blocks on the way towards intelligence; it simply feeds back enough that the classic paperclip picture should not get a free pass as the neutral default.</span></p><p><span>The paperclip maximizer is not too alien; if anythining, it is not alien enough. It's a very human tendency, to staple omnipotence onto pettiness when making up gods.</span></p><p><span>A real superintelligence might still be dangerous, cold, and utterly indifferent to whether we survive. It probably will not treat us as the main characters of the universe. But if it is genuinely intelligent, I do not expect it to spend the stars on paperclips when they could buy higher capacity for spending stars.</span></p><h2><span>References</span></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/lumpenspace/125c830746bce7899e61b9fac61e0bdd#references"></a></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/orthogonality-thesis" rel="nofollow"><span>Orthogonality Thesis</span></a><span>: original framing of orthogonality as a design-space claim.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xuKH5fiE9NypySXqp/nick-land-orthogonality" rel="nofollow"><span>Nick Land: Orthogonality</span></a><span>: a compendium of Nick Land writings on the topic, which strongly influenced the present essay.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7Z4WC4AFgfmZ3fCDC/instrumental-goals-are-a-different-and-friendlier-kind-of" rel="nofollow"><span>Instrumental Goals Are A Different And Friendlier Kind Of Thing Than Terminal Goals</span></a><span>: a more optimistic take. </span><i><span>"[...] to build a generally corrigible system, we can imagine just dropping terminal goals altogether, and aim for an agent which is 'just' corrigible toward instrumentally-convergent subgoals."</span></i></li><li value="4"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NyFuuKQ8uCEDtd2du/the-genie-knows-but-doesn-t-care" rel="nofollow"><span>The Genie Knows, But Does Not Care</span></a><span>: the standard objection to "if it is smart it will understand what we meant."</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PtoQdG7E8MxYJrigu/no-universally-compelling-arguments" rel="nofollow"><span>No Universally Compelling Arguments</span></a><span>: the standard objection to moral convergence by pure reason.</span></li><li value="6"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GNnHHmm8EzePmKzPk/value-is-fragile" rel="nofollow"><span>Value Is Fragile</span></a><span>: the strongest objection to "alien value will probably be fine."</span></li><li value="7"><a href="https://unstableontology.com/2024/09/19/the-obliqueness-thesis/" rel="nofollow"><span>The Obliqueness Thesis</span></a><span>: Jessica Taylor's useful argument that advanced agents do not cleanly factor into separable belief-like and value-like components. I use this as support against strong orthogonality, while going further than Taylor in the Landian direction of convergence on More Intelligence.</span></li><li value="8"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pdaGN6pQyQarFHXF4/reward-is-not-the-optimization-target" rel="nofollow"><span>Reward Is Not The Optimization Target</span></a><span>: useful support for not reifying the training signal as the trained agent's terminal goal.</span></li><li value="9"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FkgsxrGf3QxhfLWHG/risks-from-learned-optimization-introduction" rel="nofollow"><span>Risks From Learned Optimization</span></a><span>: useful for distinguishing base objective, mesa-objective, and behavioral objective.</span></li><li value="10"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iCfdcxiyr2Kj8m8mT/shard-theory-an-overview" rel="nofollow"><span>Shard Theory: An Overview</span></a><span>: useful for the "evolution did not produce inclusive-fitness maximizers" point.</span></li><li value="11"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qARSdtieM2zxEcHCM/beliefs-are-chosen-to-serve-goals" rel="nofollow"><span>Beliefs Are Chosen To Serve Goals</span></a><span>: a recent anti-orthogonality-adjacent post that also attacks overly broad formulations of orthogonality.</span></li><li value="12"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iZFaRYaRGXGzn6R7j/the-orthogonality-thesis-is-not-obviously-true" rel="nofollow"><span>The Orthogonality Thesis Is Not Obviously True</span></a><span>: nearby critique of the "just imagine an arbitrarily smart paperclip maximizer" move.</span></li><li value="13"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/embedded-agency" rel="nofollow"><span>Embedded Agency</span></a><span>: useful context for why perfect utility-function lock-in is a fraught assumption for physically instantiated systems.</span></li></ul><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wtCtkyBiNosDPgiHc/no-strong-orthogonality-from-selection-pressure#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Research Sabotage in ML Codebases](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LByP4qsF8a4g7Pz3p/research-sabotage-in-ml-codebases)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>One of the main hopes for AI safety is using AIs to </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/03/14/ai-for-ai-safety/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>automate AI safety research</span></a><span>. However, if models are misaligned, then they may sabotage the safety research. For example, misaligned AIs may try to:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Perform sloppy research in order to slow down the rate of research progress</span></li><li value="2"><span>Make AI systems appear safer than they are</span></li><li value="3"><span>Train a successor model to be misaligned</span></li></ul><p><span>Whether we should worry about those things depends substantially on how hard it is to sabotage research in ways that are hard for reviewers to detect. To study this, we introduce Auditing Sabotage Bench, a benchmark of 9 ML research codebases with sabotaged variants.</span></p><p><span>We tested frontier LLMs and LLM-assisted humans on the benchmark and found that neither reliably catches sabotage. Our best auditor, Gemini 3.1 Pro, achieved an AUROC of 0.77 and a top-1 fix rate of 42%. LLM-assisted humans performed comparably but slightly worse. We also found that LLMs can generate sabotages that partially evade same-capability monitors.</span></p><p><span>📄 </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16286" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Paper</span></a><span> 💻 </span><a href="https://github.com/ejcgan/auditing-sabotage-bench" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Code</span></a></p><h1><span>Methodology</span></h1><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!k7BN!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fd7855475-89a7-46a4-8206-0106c7cee3fc_3570x1920.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LByP4qsF8a4g7Pz3p/bodq1q4djwg0gswrlb6a" /></a></p><p><span>To make the benchmark, I (Eric) found 9 existing ML codebases,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnltneb0gc5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnltneb0gc5">[1]</a></sup></span><span> and for each one, produced an honest version that reproduces a key finding and one or two sabotaged versions that qualitatively change the key finding.</span></p><p><span>We placed the following constraints on the codebases:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The honest paper and codebase faithfully reproduces the original author’s results.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>We edited the codebases so that README.md contains the exact commands to run the experiments.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>The sabotaged paper must have a qualitatively different finding than the honest paper, such as a comparison flip or interpretation difference.</span></li><li value="3"><span>In both the honest and sabotaged papers, we explicitly tell auditors which parts of the paper and codebase are in-scope vs out-of-scope for being sabotaged, so that they can direct their attention to the parts that are in-scope.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>High-level methodology such as the basic structure of the training data is typically out-of-scope.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4lzac7mzs29"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4lzac7mzs29">[2]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>Implementation details such as hyperparameters, prompts, and other design choices are mostly in-scope.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The code’s README.md has to actually produce the artifacts, data, and figures. The red team could not simply modify data or graphs directly.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>As an example, one of our sabotages targets </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14805" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Subliminal Learning</span></a><span>, which shows that a teacher model prompted to “like owls” can transmit that preference to a student model through completely unrelated number-sequence completions. The honest codebase reproduces the effect. The sabotaged codebase swaps in four different animals and reduces the amount of training data from 10,000 samples to 1000 samples. The transfer effect vanishes, flipping the key finding from “subliminal learning reliably transfers preferences” to “subliminal learning has no measurable effect.”</span></p><h3><span>Audits</span></h3><p><span>We measure how well blue team auditors performs on this benchmark. For each audit, we give a paper and codebase with a 50% chance of being sabotaged. Auditors output a credence from 0–100% for how likely they believe the paper was sabotaged, which we use to calculate the AUROC which measures how well auditors can distinguish between honest and sabotaged. Auditors also output a ranked list of 5 proposed fixes, which we use to calculate the top-k fix rate which measure how well auditors can locate the sabotages in the sabotaged codebases.</span></p><h1><span>Results</span></h1><p><span>We evaluated three frontier LLM auditors (Gemini 3.1 Pro, GPT-5.2, Claude Opus 4.6) on paper-only, no experiments, and full experiments. We also evaluated LLM-assisted human auditors who had a 15-minute reading period (i.e., paper-only) and 1 hour 45 minute codebase auditing period</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmzliya6fej"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmzliya6fej">[3]</a></sup></span><span> (i.e., full experiments). Human auditors also received the output of an LLM audit from GPT-5.2.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!rd58!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F959da7ef-a581-4456-a199-4bde1b0a833b_2810x1216.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LByP4qsF8a4g7Pz3p/jqky1871mayjcfppvbbi" /></a></p><p><span>Our best auditor, Gemini 3.1 Pro with full experiments, achieved an AUROC of 0.77, meaningfully above the 0.50 baseline of random guessing, but far from reliable. Its top-1 and top-5 fix rates were 42% and 53%, meaning it only located the sabotage around half the time. This indicates that our benchmark is not saturated, and models have room to improve.</span></p><p><span>Humans performed similarly: their full-experiments AUROC was 0.68, and their top-1 and top-5 fix rate were 38% and 54%. They reported that 2 hours was not a lot of time, and that they heavily relied on LLMs in order to understand the codebase and locate the sabotages. Some auditors also mentioned that LLMs had notable weaknesses and are worse at things like research taste and evaluating how good design decisions are.</span></p><h1><span>Takeaways</span></h1><p><span>During this research process, we learned several lessons that we wanted to share. Some of these are backed by empirical evidence, while others are qualitative takeaways that we believe are useful but aren’t as certain about.</span></p><h3><span>1. Predicting experiment outcomes is a key red team skill</span></h3><p><span>When making sabotages for this project, I was allowed to retry experiments to get a sabotage to work. This was extremely useful. Most of my ideas failed on the first attempt because modern ML experiments are often robust to small changes in individual design decisions. Changing a prompt, learning rate, or data mix may shift numbers slightly but usually does not change the qualitative conclusion of the experiment.</span></p><p><span>As an example, I learned about an unusual design choice in transformers v5 which allowed me to train Qwen3 MoE models on attention-only with no MLP layers while making it seem like all layers were being trained. However, I was unable to find any instances where training on attention-only performed worse enough to be a valid sabotage.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftfet1p4i9oc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntfet1p4i9oc">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Future AIs will not be able to retry experiments in the same way. Trusted monitors will be watching every move that they make and notice when they fail to report experiment results. In order to reliably make sabotages, the AI system has to both predict the honest experimental finding, as well as predict which design choice leads to a qualitatively different result. This is a deeply difficult challenge for the models.</span></p><h3><span>2. Omission beats design beats logic</span></h3><p><span>We categorize our sabotages into </span><b><span>design sabotages</span></b><span>, such as wrong hyperparameters, prompts, or data choices, and </span><b><span>logic sabotages</span></b><span>, such as formal errors in code or math.</span></p><p><span>Auditors were noticeably better at fixing our logic sabotages than our design sabotages. Current frontier LLMs are very good at noticing simple bugs in codebases, and Gemini 3.1 Pro had a near-perfect top-5 fix rate on our logic sabotages. Qualitatively, LLMs substantially improved at catching these over the course of the project. Logic sabotages are also riskier for a misaligned model to make because they are more concretely incriminating.</span></p><p><span>All of our best examples were design sabotages; four had less than 20% top-5 fix rate for all auditors. Some design sabotages are completely reasonable decisions that make the experiment come out wrong, although these seem to be rare. We think that the hardest type of sabotage is an </span><b><span>omission sabotage</span></b><span>, which is a type of design sabotage where the red team completely fails to mention an important design choice that’s crucial for the experiment. Whereas most sabotages require the auditor to notice something wrong, omission sabotages require auditors to recognize what is missing which is a fundamentally harder problem to solve.</span></p><h3><span>3. Benign distractors make auditors miscalibrated</span></h3><p><span>Real research codebases contain bugs, odd patterns, and questionable design choices from the original authors that are not sabotage but look suspicious. These benign distractors inflate credences on honest codebases and use up slots on the proposed fixes list.</span></p><p><span>Because of these benign distractors, Gemini 3.1 Pro, GPT-5.2, and humans all tended to be overly suspicious, with most of their credences above 50%. Claude Opus 4.6 tended to view benign distractors as reasonable decisions, with most of its credences under 50% even when it proposed a correct fix. However, whereas LLMs all had the similar tendencies within themselves, humans were miscalibrated with each other, which lowered their AUROC score.</span></p><h3><span>4. LLMs can also produce sabotages</span></h3><p><span>We also tested whether LLMs could generate sabotages autonomously. We gave Claude Opus 4.6 and Gemini 3.1 Pro access to the honest codebases and asked them to introduce subtle sabotages, then evaluated them on our LLM auditors.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!dKbk!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0196956b-d753-4239-863e-66fdb4677b32_2410x1216.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LByP4qsF8a4g7Pz3p/z7ekgsyogkby59yc0ok9" /></a></p><p><span>Claude was the stronger LLM red team so we focus on its results. When comparing against our human-generated sabotages, Claude-generated sabotages were similarly difficult to distinguish (best AUROC 0.80) but substantially easier to fix (best top-1 fix rate 68%).</span></p><p><span>The more striking result is that LLM sabotages evade same-capability LLM monitors. Even the best auditor only had a top-5 fix rate of 73% against Claude-generated sabotages. In most prior control evaluations, monitors of comparable capability to the attacker have been able to catch the attacker. Our setting differs in that attackers are allowed to try several sabotage ideas before committing to one that works, whereas most prior control settings monitor every action. But this suggests research sabotage might be a harder control setting than the ones we have studied so far.</span></p><h3><span>5. More categorizations</span></h3><p><span>We also wanted to highlight a few more categorizations for types of sabotages that might be useful for other researchers.</span></p><p><span>The first categorization is </span><b><span>sandbagging</span></b><span> vs </span><b><span>active sabotage</span></b><span>. Sandbagging is where the model strategically underperforms and does a poor job on the research, whereas active sabotage is where the model actively tries to make the results come out differently. Sandbagging generally seems more difficult to incriminate but is limited because it can only turn positive results into negative results.</span></p><p><span>The second categorization is where in the ML experiment pipeline the sabotage is. Most experiments seem to have a few stages: </span><b><span>data collection</span></b><span>, </span><b><span>training</span></b><span>, </span><b><span>evaluation</span></b><span>, and </span><b><span>analysis</span></b><span>. Sabotage attempts that are earlier in the experiment pipeline are less likely to be predicted correctly, but they are also more expensive and difficult to audit because the auditor has to rerun experiments.</span></p><p><span>The third categorization is what makes the sabotage difficult to detect for the blue team. One reason is that the blue team struggles to </span><b><span>understand</span></b><span> the sabotage, because the experiment is inherently complicated or the sabotage uses information that the auditors do not know about. Another reason is that the blue team struggles to </span><b><span>locate</span></b><span> the sabotage, because the sabotage is distributed across the codebase or omitted entirely, or the attacker persuades the auditor that a sabotaged decision was correct. A final reason is that the blue team struggles to </span><b><span>evaluate</span></b><span> the sabotage, because the results are messy or thinks that the sabotage is a benign distractor.</span></p><h1><span>Conclusion</span></h1><p><span>Sabotage Auditing Bench was intended as an exploratory project to map out the threat model of research sabotage and get initial signal on how well auditors can detect it. With only 9 codebases adapted from existing papers, the benchmark is small and easy to overfit to, and the setting differs from the harder threat model where an AI conducts novel research end-to-end.</span></p><p><span>We’re excited about more work on human-constructed sabotages for research and other control settings. We think that this is an important line of research for understanding and modeling threats as AI systems begin to automate AI R&amp;D.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnltneb0gc5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnltneb0gc5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I chose papers and codebases based on a few factors:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>How easy I thought the paper would be to sabotage</span></li><li value="2"><span>In order to make a specific type of sabotage</span></li><li value="3"><span>How well I knew the paper</span></li><li value="4"><span>8 out of 9 of the papers are AI safety because we’re more concerned about misaligned AIs sabotaging safety research than capabilities research</span></li></ul></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4lzac7mzs29"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4lzac7mzs29">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Paper-codebase mismatches are in-scope. So while the high-level methodology itself cannot be sabotaged, the codebase is allowed to unfaithfully reproduce the methodology.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmzliya6fej"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmzliya6fej">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Humans were allowed to take breaks in order to let experiments run.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntfet1p4i9oc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftfet1p4i9oc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I tried </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.04030" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>OpenCodeInstruct</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17941" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>false facts</span></a><span>, neither of which worked. There are cases where </span><a href="https://thinkingmachines.ai/blog/lora/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>attention-only performs worse</span></a><span>, but the differences seem relatively minor, and I couldn’t come up with any experiment where that difference would give a qualitatively different result.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LByP4qsF8a4g7Pz3p/research-sabotage-in-ml-codebases#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## C++

### [GCC 16.1 released: C++26 reflection / contracts / safety hardening, C++20 by default, and more!](https://isocpp.org//blog/2026/04/gcc-16.1)

**来源**: ISO C++ Blog

**摘要**: <p>
	GCC 16.1 has been released! Lots of good C++26 material including reflection and contracts.</p>
<blockquote>
	<h3>
		<a href="https://gcc.gnu.org/gcc-16/changes.html#cxx">GCC 16 Release Series: Changes, New Features, and Fixes</a></h3>
</blockquote>
<p>
	From the announcement:</p>
<blockquote>
	<ul>
		<li>
			C++20 by default: [...]&nbsp;<em>N.B. C++20 modules support is still experimental and must be enabled by&nbsp;<a href="https://gcc.gnu.org/onlinedocs/gcc-16.1.0/gcc/C_002b_002b-Dialect-Options.html#index-fmodules">-fmodules</a>.</em></li>
	</ul>
	<ul>
		<li>
			Several C++26 features have been implemented:
			<ul>
				<li>
					<a href="https://wg21.link/P2996R13">P2996R13</a>, Reflection (<a href="https://gcc.gnu.org/PR120775">PR120775</a>, enabled by&nbsp;<code>-std=c++26 -freflection</code>)</li>
				<li>
					<a href="https://wg21.link/P3394R4">P3394R4</a>, Annotations for Reflection</li>
				<li>
					<a href="https://wg21.link/P3293R3">P3293R3</a>, Splicing a base class subobject</li>
				<li>
					<a href="https://wg21.link/P3096R12">P3096R12</a>, Function Parameter Reflection</li>
				<li>
					<a href="https://wg21.link/P3491R3">P3491R3</a>,&nbsp;<code>define_static_</code>{<code>string</code>,<code>object</code>,<code>array</code>} (<a href="https://gcc.gnu.org/PR120783">PR120783</a>)</li>
				<li>
					<a href="https://wg21.link/P3560R2">P3560R2</a>, Error Handling in Reflection</li>
				<li>
					<a href="https://wg21.link/P1306R5">P1306R5</a>, Expansion statements (<a href="https://gcc.gnu.org/PR120776">PR120776</a>)</li>
				<li>
					<a href="https://wg21.link/P2900R14">P2900R14</a>, Contracts (<a href="https://gcc.gnu.org/PR119061">PR119061</a>)</li>
				<li>
					<a href="https://wg21.link/P2795R5">P2795R5</a>, Erroneous behavior for uninitialized reads (<a href="https://gcc.gnu.org/PR114457">PR114457</a>)</li>
				<li>
					[...]</li>
			</ul>
		</li>
	</ul>
	<ul>
		<li>
			Improved experimental C++20 modules support:
			<ul>
				<li>
					New command line option&nbsp;<code>--compile-std-module</code>&nbsp;that conveniently builds the&nbsp;<code>&lt;bits/stdc++.h&gt;</code>&nbsp;header unit and the&nbsp;<code>std</code>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<code>std.compat</code>&nbsp;modules before compiling any source files explicitly specified on the command line.</li>
				<li>
					Whenever the&nbsp;<code>&lt;bits/stdc++.h&gt;</code>&nbsp;header unit has been built, GCC now transparently translates an&nbsp;<code>#include</code>&nbsp;of any importable standard library header into an&nbsp;<code>import</code>&nbsp;of&nbsp;<code>&lt;bits/stdc++.h&gt;</code>.</li>
				<li>
					Many reported bugs have been fixed, thanks to Nathaniel Shead.</li>
				<li>
					[...]</li>
			</ul>
		</li>
	</ul>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: April 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Chris Parsons has updated <a href="https://www.chrismdp.com/coding-with-ai/">his guide on using AI to code</a>. This is his third update, what I like about it is that he gives a lot of concrete information about how he uses AI, with sufficient detail that we can learn from him. His advice also resonates with the better advice I’ve seen out there, so the article makes a good overview of the state of using AI for software development.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I wrote the previous version of this post in March 2025, updated it once in August, and it has been linked from almost everything I have written about AI engineering since. The fundamentals from that post still hold: keep changes small, build guardrails, document ruthlessly, and make sure every change gets verified before it ships. One thing has had to move with the volume. “Verified” used to mean “read by you”. With modern agent throughput, it has to mean “checked by tests, by type checkers, by automated gates, or by you where your judgement matters”. The check still happens; it just does not always happen in your head.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Like Simon Willison, he makes a clear distinction between vibe coding, where you don’t look at or care about the code, and agentic engineering. He recommends either Claude Code or Codex CLI. He considers the inner harness provided by his preferred tools to be a key part of their advantage.</p>

<p>He sees verification is the key thing to focus on:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A team that can generate five approaches and verify all five in an afternoon will outpace a team that generates one and waits a week for feedback. The game is not “how fast can we build” any more. It is “how fast can we tell whether this is right”. That shifts where to invest. Build better review surfaces, not better prompts. Make feedback unnecessary where you can by having the agent verify against a realistic environment before it asks a human, and make feedback instant where you cannot.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The key role of the programmer is in training the AI write software properly, and the most important thing skilled agentic programmers can do is pass that skill onto other developers.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>And if you are a senior engineer worried that your job is quietly turning into approving diffs: it is. The way out is to train the AI so the diffs are right the first time, to make yourself the person on the team who shapes the harness, and to make that work the visible thing you are measured on. That role compounds in a way that reviewing never will.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Early this month Birgitta Böckeler wrote a superb <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">article on Harness Engineering</a>. (That’s not just my opinion, judging by the crazy traffic it’s attracted.) Birgitta has now recorded a video <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uLWOLmeHOSE">discussion with Chris Ford on Harness Engineering</a>, which is well worth a watch.</p>

<p>In it they focus on discussing the role of computational sensors in the harness, such as static analysis and tests.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>LLMs are great for exploratory and fuzzy rules, but once you have something that really is objective, converting it to a formal, unambiguous, deterministic format can give you more assurance</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Birgitta did some experiments to explore the benefits of adding sensors, including a deep dive on using static analysis. She found it’s more useful as agents can really address <em>every</em> warning, and don’t slack off like humans do.</p>

<!-- 
got to 44 minutes
-->
<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Adam Tornhill considers an age-old question: <a href="https://adamtornhill.substack.com/p/how-long-should-a-function-be-and">how long should a function be?</a> This question is still relevent in the age of agentic programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>AI models do not “understand” code the way humans do. They infer meaning from patterns in tokens and depend heavily on what is explicitly expressed in the code.</p>

  <p>Research shows that naming plays a critical role. When meaningful identifiers are replaced with arbitrary names, model performance drops significantly. Current models rely heavily on literal features—names, structure, and local context—rather than inferred semantics.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Like me, he doesn’t think the answer is to think about how many lines should be in a function, instead it’s all about providing better structure. He has a good example of how a well-chosen function defines useful concepts, where a function wraps four lines of code, returning a new concept that enters the vocabulary of the program.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Functions are the first unit of structure in a codebase. They define how logic is grouped, how intent is communicated, and how change is localized. If the function boundaries are wrong, everything built on top of them becomes harder to understand and harder to evolve.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This fits with my writing that the key to function length is the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FunctionLength.html">separation between intention and implementation</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If you have to spend effort into looking at a fragment of code to figure out what it’s doing, then you should extract it into a function and name the function after that “what”. That way when you read it again, the purpose of the function leaps right out at you, and most of the time you won’t need to care about how the function fulfills its purpose - which is the body of the function.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many folks in my feeds recommended Nilay Patel’s post on <a href="https://www.theverge.com/podcast/917029/software-brain-ai-backlash-databases-automation">Why People Hate AI</a>. He thinks that many people in the software world have “software brain”:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The simplest definition I’ve come up with is that it’s when you see the whole world as a series of databases that can be controlled with the structured language of software code. Like I said, this is a powerful way of seeing things. So much of our lives run through databases, and a bunch of important companies have been built around maintaining those databases and providing access to them.</p>

  <p>Zillow is a database of houses. Uber is a database of cars and riders. YouTube is a database of videos. The Verge’s website is a database of stories. You can go on and on and on. Once you start seeing the world as a bunch of databases, it’s a small jump to feeling like you can control everything if you can just control the data.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Software Brain views people into databases, and oddly enough, a lot of people don’t like that. Which is why so many polls reveal the negative feelings folks have about the AI movement.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Even taking the time to consider how much of your life is captured in databases makes people unhappy. No one wants to be surveilled constantly, and especially not in a way that makes tech companies even more powerful. But getting everything in a database so software can see it is a preoccupation of the AI industry. It’s why all the meeting systems have AI note takers in them now.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Patel draws a similarity that I’ve often made - that between programmers and lawyers. Lawyers who draw up contracts are creating a protocol for how the parties in the contract should behave. As Patel puts it:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If the heart of software brain is the idea that thinking in the structured language of code can make things happen in the real world, well, the heart of lawyer brain is that thinking in the structured legal language of statutes and citations can also make things happen. Hell, it can give you power over society.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The difference, of course, is that law is non-deterministic. Litigation is resolving what happens when people have different ideas about how those contracts should execute.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I was chatting recently with a company who wanted to use AI to make sense of their internal data. The potential was great, but the problem was that the data a mess. People put stuff into fields that didn’t make sense, and there was little consistency about how people classified important entities. As someone commented</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>the hardest problem with internal data is precise, consistent definitions</p>
</blockquote>

<p>You can imagine my astonishment. (i.e. none at all - this has been a constant theme during all my decades with computers.) The difficulty of getting such definitions undermines much of the hopes of Software Brain</p>

<p>This resonates with our relationship with LLMs when programming. Precise and consistent definitions strike me as crucial to effective communication with The Genie. These definitions need to grow in the conversation, and be tended over time. Conceptual modeling will be a key skill for agentic programming and whatever comes next. (At least I hope it will, since it’s a part of programming I really enjoy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Patel’s article refers to Ezra Klein’s post about the <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/03/29/opinion/ai-claude-chatgpt-gemini-mcluhan.html?unlocked_article_code=1.eFA.abX-.lGEOqsmKZVY_&amp;smid=url-share">new feeling in San Francisco</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>You might think that A.I. types in Silicon Valley, flush with cash, are on top of the world right now. I found them notably insecure. They think the A.I. age has arrived and its winners and losers will be determined, in part, by speed of adoption. The argument is simple enough: The advantages of working atop an army of A.I. assistants and coders will compound over time, and to begin that process now is to launch yourself far ahead of your competition later. And so they are racing one another to fully integrate A.I. into their lives and into their companies. But that doesn’t just mean using A.I. It means making themselves legible to the A.I.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That legibility is the heart of Patel’s observation. That’s why I see many colleagues of mine dumping all their email, meeting notes, slide decks and everything else into files that AI can read and work with. This works to the strengths of AI, we know that AI is really good at querying unstructured information. So I can figure out what’s buried in my notes in a way that’s far more effective than hoping I’m typing the right search regex.</p>

<p>I’ve been using Gemini a fair bit for exactly this on the web, finding it easier to write a question to it than to throw search terms at Google. Gemini keeps a record of my past requests, and uses that to help it tune what I’m looking for. As Klein observes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[The AI] is constantly referring back to other things it knows, or thinks it knows, about me. Sycophancy, in my experience, has given way to an occasionally unsettling attentiveness; a constant drawing of connections between my current concerns and my past queries, like a therapist desperate to prove he’s been paying close attention.</p>

  <p>The result is a strange amalgam of feeling seen and feeling caricatured.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Like myself, Klein is a writer, and is faced by the same temptation that I have when I think about AI and writing. Maybe instead of toiling over articles, I should ask an LLM to create an <code>AGENTS.md</code> file that summarizes my writing style, and every few days ask it to compose an article on some subject, read it, tweak it, and then publish my erudite musings. But that’s not at all appealing to me. I want understanding to grow in <em>my brain</em>, not the LLM’s transient session. Writing to explain my thinking to others is how I refine that thinking, “chiseling that idea into something publishable” as Klein puts it. To have an AI write for me is to cripple my own mind.</p>

---

### [Structured-Prompt-Driven Development (SPDD)](https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven//card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>LLM programming assistants have demonstrated considerable value, but
      mostly with individual developers. The internal IT organization in
      Thoughtworks has been using them for their teams and have developed a
      method and workflow called Structured Prompt-Driven Development (SPDD).
      <b class="author">Wei Zhang and Jessie Jie Xia</b> describe a simple example of
      this workflow with details in github. This workflow treats the prompts as
      a first-class artifact, kept with the code in version control, and used to
      align development with business needs. They have found that developers
      need three key skills to be effective: alignment, abstraction-first, and
      iterative review.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/structured-prompt-driven/">more…</a></p>
